Prompt

A. 5
B. 6
C. 8
D. 9

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
求中位数的步骤如下：

1. **排序**：将给定的样本数据按从小到大的顺序重新排列，得到：$4, 5, 6, 8, 12$。
2. **确定位置**：该样本共有 $n=5$ 个数据，个数为奇数。根据中位数的定义，中位数是位于正中间位置的那个数，即第 $\frac{n+1}{2} = \frac{5+1}{2} = 3$ 位。
3. **取值**：在排序后的数据中，第3个数是 $6$。

因此，这组样本数据的中位数为 $6$，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}

gaokao_000_001 ✓ A A 1531 57.5 26.6 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

2. 已知平面向量\vec{a}，\vec{b}不共线，且2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}，则（ ）
A. x=2, y=-3
B. x=-2, y=3
C. x=2, y=3
D. x=-2, y=-3

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **分析已知条件**：题目给出平面向量 $\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ **不共线**。根据平面向量基本定理，任意两个不共线的平面向量可以作为该平面的一组基底，且平面内任一向量用这组基底线性表示的形式是**唯一**的。

2. **利用向量相等的充要条件**：若两个向量用同一组不共线的基底表示，则它们相等当且仅当对应基底的系数分别相等。
   已知等式为：
   $$2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$$

3. **比较对应系数**：
   - 比较 $\vec{a}$ 的系数：左边为 $2$，右边为 $x$，故 $x = 2$。
   - 比较 $\vec{b}$ 的系数：左边为 $y$，右边为 $-3$，故 $y = -3$。

4. **得出结论**：解得 $x=2$，$y=-3$。对照选项，与选项 A 完全一致。

**最终答案：**
\boxed{A}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple choice question (单选题)
   - **Topic:** Plane vectors (平面向量)
   - **Given:** Vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear (不共线). Equation: $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.
   - **Options:**
     A. $x=2, y=-3$
     B. $x=-2, y=3$
     C. $x=2, y=3$
     D. $x=-2, y=-3$
   - **Task:** Solve step-by-step and put the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Identify Key Mathematical Concepts:**
   - Vectors $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear, which means they are linearly independent in a 2D plane.
   - For linearly independent vectors, if $c_1\vec{a} + c_2\vec{b} = d_1\vec{a} + d_2\vec{b}$, then $c_1 = d_1$ and $c_2 = d_2$. This is a fundamental property of vector basis.
   - The given equation is $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$.

3.  **Apply Concepts to Solve:**
   - Rearrange/Compare coefficients of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ on both sides.
   - Coefficient of $\vec{a}$: Left side = 2, Right side = $x$. So, $x = 2$.
   - Coefficient of $\vec{b}$: Left side = $y$, Right side = $-3$. So, $y = -3$.
   - Therefore, $x=2$ and $y=-3$.

4.  **Match with Options:**
   - Option A: $x=2, y=-3$ matches exactly.
   - Options B, C, D do not match.

5.  **Formulate Step-by-Step Reasoning (for output):**
   - 指出已知条件：$\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ 不共线，说明它们是平面上的一组基底，具有线性无关性。
   - 根据平面向量基本定理（或向量相等的充要条件），若两个向量用同一组不共线的基底线性表示，则对应系数相等。
   - 将等式 $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$ 两边按基底 $\vec{a}, \vec{b}$ 比较系数。
   - $\vec{a}$ 的系数：$2 = x \Rightarrow x = 2$。
   - $\vec{b}$ 的系数：$y = -3 \Rightarrow y = -3$。
   - 对照选项，A 符合。
   - 最终答案：A。

6.  **Check for Pitfalls/Edge Cases:**
   - Is there any trick? No, it's a standard application of the vector basis theorem.
   - "不共线" (non-collinear) is crucial because if they were collinear, the coefficients wouldn't necessarily match uniquely. But since they are non-collinear, the representation is unique.
   - The steps are solid.

7.  **Draft Response (Mental Refinement):**
   根据平面向量基本定理，已知平面向量 $\vec{a}$ 与 $\vec{b}$ 不共线，则它们可以作为平面的一组基底。对于平面内的任意向量，用这组基底线性表示的形式是唯一的。
   由等式 $2\vec{a} + y\vec{b} = x\vec{a} - 3\vec{b}$，根据向量相等的充要条件（对应基底系数相等），可得：
   $\begin{cases} x = 2 \\ y = -3 \end{cases}$
   因此，$x=2$，$y=-3$。
   对照选项，正确答案为 A。
   最终答案：\boxed{A}

Prompt

3. 已知集合A={sin\frac{7\pi}{6}, cos\frac{5\pi}{3}, tan\frac{5\pi}{4}}, B={-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}，则A \cap B = （ ）
A. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}}
B. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, 1}
C. {-\frac{1}{2}, 1}
D. {-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **计算集合 $A$ 中的元素值**：
   - $\sin\frac{7\pi}{6} = \sin\left(\pi + \frac{\pi}{6}\right) = -\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = -\frac{1}{2}$
   - $\cos\frac{5\pi}{3} = \cos\left(2\pi - \frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \cos\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{1}{2}$
   - $\tan\frac{5\pi}{4} = \tan\left(\pi + \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \tan\frac{\pi}{4} = 1$
   - 因此，集合 $A = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

2. **写出集合 $B$ 的元素**：
   - 已知 $B = \left\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

3. **求交集 $A \cap B$**：
   - 交集是指同时属于集合 $A$ 和集合 $B$ 的元素。
   - 对比 $A = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$ 与 $B = \left\{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$，
   - 公共元素为 $-\frac{1}{2}$ 和 $1$。
   - 故 $A \cap B = \left\{-\frac{1}{2}, 1\right\}$。

4. **对照选项**：
   - 计算结果与选项 **C** 完全一致。

**最终答案：**
\boxed{C}

gaokao_000_003 ✓ D D 1694 54.9 30.9 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

4. 曲线y=5x+8lnx在点(1,5)处的切线方程为（ ）
A. y=3x+2
B. y=5x
C. y=8x-3
D. y=13x-8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **求函数的导数**  
   已知曲线方程为 $y = 5x + 8\ln x$。  
   对 $x$ 求导得：  
   $$y' = (5x)' + (8\ln x)' = 5 + \frac{8}{x}$$

2. **计算切点处的切线斜率**  
   切点横坐标为 $x=1$，将其代入导数表达式：  
   $$k = y'\big|_{x=1} = 5 + \frac{8}{1} = 13$$  
   因此，切线的斜率为 $13$。

3. **利用点斜式写出切线方程**  
   已知切点为 $(1, 5)$，斜率 $k=13$，根据点斜式 $y - y_0 = k(x - x_0)$ 可得：  
   $$y - 5 = 13(x - 1)$$

4. **化简方程并与选项对比**  
   $$y - 5 = 13x - 13$$  
   $$y = 13x - 8$$

\boxed{D}

gaokao_000_005 ✓ B B 7489 53.5 140.0 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

6. 已知函数f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}的最大值为1，则a = （ ）
A. \frac{1}{2}
B. 1
C. \frac{3}{2}
D. 2

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **求导数**
   函数为 $f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+a}$，利用商的求导法则：
   $$f'(x) = \frac{(x+2)'(e^x+a) - (x+2)(e^x+a)'}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{1\cdot(e^x+a) - (x+2)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}$$
   化简分子得：
   $$f'(x) = \frac{e^x + a - xe^x - 2e^x}{(e^x+a)^2} = \frac{a - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x+a)^2}$$

2. **分析极值点条件**
   函数在某点取得最大值，该点必为驻点（导数为0）或边界点。由于 $x \to \pm\infty$ 时 $f(x) \to 0$ 或 $-\infty$，最大值必在驻点处取得。
   令 $f'(x)=0$，得：
   $$(x+1)e^x = a \quad \cdots\cdots ①$$

3. **利用最大值为1列方程**
   设最大值在 $x_0$ 处取得，则 $f(x_0)=1$，即：
   $$\frac{x_0+2}{e^{x_0}+a} = 1 \Rightarrow x_0+2 = e^{x_0} + a \quad \cdots\cdots ②$$

4. **联立求解**
   将①式代入②式，消去 $a$：
   $$x_0+2 = e^{x_0} + (x_0+1)e^{x_0}$$
   $$x_0+2 = e^{x_0}(1 + x_0 + 1) = e^{x_0}(x_0+2)$$
   移项整理得：
   $$(x_0+2)(1 - e^{x_0}) = 0$$
   若 $x_0+2=0$，则 $x_0=-2$，此时 $f(-2)=0 \neq 1$，与最大值为1矛盾，故舍去。
   因此必有 $1 - e^{x_0} = 0$，解得 $x_0 = 0$。

5. **求参数 $a$ 并验证**
   将 $x_0=0$ 代入①式：
   $$a = (0+1)e^0 = 1$$
   **验证：** 当 $a=1$ 时，$f(x)=\frac{x+2}{e^x+1}$，$f'(x)=\frac{1-(x+1)e^x}{(e^x+1)^2}$。
   令 $g(x)=(x+1)e^x$，则 $g'(x)=(x+2)e^x$。当 $x>-2$ 时 $g(x)$ 单调递增。
   因 $g(0)=1$，故 $x=0$ 是 $f'(x)=0$ 的唯一解。
   当 $x<0$ 时，$g(x)<1 \Rightarrow f'(x)>0$；当 $x>0$ 时，$g(x)>1 \Rightarrow f'(x)<0$。
   所以 $x=0$ 是极大值点，也是全局最大值点，且 $f(0)=\frac{2}{2}=1$，完全符合题意。

综上所述，$a=1$，对应选项 B。

\boxed{B}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze the User Input:**
   - **Question:** Given the function $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$, its maximum value is 1. Find the value of $a$.
   - **Options:** A. $1/2$, B. $1$, C. $3/2$, D. $2$
   - **Task:** Solve step-by-step, put the final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Understand the Problem:**
   - We have a function $f(x) = \frac{x+2}{e^x + a}$.
   - We are told that the maximum value of $f(x)$ is 1.
   - We need to find the constant $a$.
   - Since it's a maximum value problem, we should look at the derivative $f'(x)$ to find critical points.
   - At the maximum point $x_0$, we must have $f'(x_0) = 0$ and $f(x_0) = 1$.
   - Also, the denominator $e^x + a$ must be non-zero. Usually, for such problems, $a > 0$ to avoid singularities, but we'll check.

3.  **Compute the Derivative:**
   - $f(x) = \frac{u}{v}$ where $u = x+2$, $v = e^x + a$.
   - $f'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{1 \cdot (e^x + a) - (x+2) \cdot e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$
   - Simplify numerator: $e^x + a - xe^x - 2e^x = a - xe^x - e^x = a - (x+1)e^x$.
   - So, $f'(x) = \frac{a - (x+1)e^x}{(e^x + a)^2}$.

4.  **Find Critical Points:**
   - Set $f'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow a - (x+1)e^x = 0 \Rightarrow (x+1)e^x = a$.
   - Let $g(x) = (x+1)e^x$. We need to solve $g(x) = a$.
   - Analyze $g(x)$: $g'(x) = e^x + (x+1)e^x = x e^x$.
   - $g'(x) = 0 \Rightarrow x = 0$.
   - For $x < 0$, $g'(x) < 0$ (decreasing).
   - For $x > 0$, $g'(x) > 0$ (increasing).
   - So $g(x)$ has a global minimum at $x=0$, with $g(0) = 1$.
   - Thus, $g(x) \ge 1$ for all real $x$.
   - For the equation $(x+1)e^x = a$ to have a solution, we must have $a \ge 1$.
   - If $a = 1$, the only solution is $x = 0$.
   - If $a > 1$, there are two solutions, one $x_1 < 0$ and one $x_2 > 0$.
   - Let's check the sign of $f'(x)$ to determine max/min.
   - $f'(x) = \frac{a - g(x)}{(e^x+a)^2}$. The denominator is always positive.
   - So the sign of $f'(x)$ is the sign of $a - g(x)$.
   - If $a > 1$, $g(x)$ goes from $\infty$ (as $x \to -\infty$) down to 1 at $x=0$, then up to $\infty$.
     - For $x < x_1$, $g(x) > a \Rightarrow f'(x) < 0$.
     - For $x_1 < x < x_2$, $g(x) < a \Rightarrow f'(x) > 0$.
     - For $x > x_2$, $g(x) > a \Rightarrow f'(x) < 0$.

Prompt
请解答以下数学题（单选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

7. 一百零八塔位于宁夏回族自治区青铜峡市，以其独特的建筑格局和深厚的历史文化闻名遐迩。该塔群共有108座塔，依山势自上而下排成12行，将第i行中塔的座数记为a_i(i=1,2,...,12)，其中a_1=1, a_2=a_3=3, a_4=a_5=5，且a_6, a_7, ..., a_{12}是一个首项为7，公差为2的等差数列。将a_1, a_2, ..., a_{12}分为6组，每组2个数，使得每组的2个数之和可构成一个项数为6且公差为d(d>0)的等差数列，则d = （ ）
A. 2
B. 4
C. 6
D. 8

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理过程：**

1. **列出数列各项并验证总和**
   根据题意，12行塔的座数依次为：
   $a_1=1$，$a_2=3$，$a_3=3$，$a_4=5$，$a_5=5$；
   $a_6, a_7, \dots, a_{12}$ 是首项为7、公差为2的等差数列，共7项，依次为：$7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19$。
   将这12个数全部相加验证：
   $1 + 3 + 3 + 5 + 5 + 7 + 9 + 11 + 13 + 15 + 17 + 19 = 108$，与题目“共有108座塔”一致。
   因此，待配对的数字集合为：$\{1, 3, 3, 5, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15, 17, 19\}$。

2. **建立等差数列和的方程**
   将这12个数分为6组，每组2个数，设每组的和依次为 $S_1, S_2, S_3, S_4, S_5, S_6$。
   由题意，$S_1, S_2, \dots, S_6$ 构成公差为 $d(d>0)$ 的等差数列。
   这6个和的总和等于所有塔数的总和，即：
   $$S_1 + S_2 + S_3 + S_4 + S_5 + S_6 = 108$$
   根据等差数列求和公式：
   $$6S_1 + \frac{6 \times 5}{2}d = 108 \implies 6S_1 + 15d = 108$$
   两边同除以3，得：
   $$2S_1 + 5d = 36$$

3. **分析 $d$ 的可能取值**
   由于 $S_1$ 和 $d$ 均为正整数，由 $2S_1 = 36 - 5d > 0$ 可得 $5d < 36 \implies d \leq 7$。
   又因为 $2S_1$ 是偶数，$36$ 是偶数，所以 $5d$ 必为偶数，故 $d$ 必须是**偶数**。
   结合选项，$d$ 的可能取值为 $2, 4, 6$（$d=8$ 超出范围，直接排除）。

4. **代入验证排除**
   * **若 $d=2$**：代入方程得 $2S_1 + 10 = 36 \implies S_1 = 13$。
     此时6个和为：$13, 15, 17, 19, 21, 23$。
     集合中最大数为19，要得到和为23，需配对 $(19, 4)$ 或 $(17, 6)$ 等，但集合中**没有4和6**，故 $d \neq 2$。
   * **若 $d=6$**：代入方程得 $2S_1 + 30 = 36 \implies S_1 = 3$。
     此时6个和为：$3, 9, 15, 21, 27, 33$。
     集合中最小数为1，要得到和为3，需配对 $(1, 2)$，但集合中**没有2**，故 $d \neq 6$。
   * **若 $d=4$**：代入方程得 $2S_1 + 20 = 36 \implies S_1 = 8$。
     此时6个和为：$8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28$。
     尝试配对：
     $28 = 19 + 9$
     $24 = 17 + 7$
     $20 = 15 + 5$
     $16 = 13 + 3$
     $12 = 11 + 1$
     $8 = 5 + 3$
     所有数字恰好用完，且每组和恰好构成公差为4的等差数列，**符合题意**。

综上所述，公差 $d=4$。

\boxed{B}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Understand the User's Request:**
    - The user provides a multiple-choice math problem in Chinese.
    - The problem is about the "108 Pagodas" in Ningxia.
    - There are 12 rows, with the number of pagodas in row $i$ denoted as $a_i$ for $i=1, 2, \dots, 12$.
    - Given: $a_1 = 1$, $a_2 = a_3 = 3$, $a_4 = a_5 = 5$.
    - $a_6, a_7, \dots, a_{12}$ is an arithmetic progression (AP) with first term 7 and common difference 2.
    - The 12 numbers $a_1, \dots, a_{12}$ are divided into 6 pairs (groups of 2 numbers each).

8. 设U={(x_1, x_2, x_3)|x_i \in \{-2,-1,1,2"}, i=1,2,3}\}为空间中64个点构成的集合，点P(1,1,1)，记样本空间\Omega = C_U P，从\Omega中随机取一个点，定义随机变量X如下：对\Omega中的每个点A(x_1, x_2, x_3)，令X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3，则X的数学期望值为（ ）
A. -\frac{1}{21}
B. -\frac{1}{63}
C. 0
D. \frac{1}{7}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
**逐步推理：**

1. **确定全集与样本空间的大小**
   - 集合 $U = \{(x_1, x_2, x_3) \mid x_i \in \{-2, -1, 1, 2\}, i=1,2,3\}$，每个坐标有 4 种取值，且相互独立，故 $|U| = 4^3 = 64$。
   - 符号 $C_U P$ 表示点 $P$ 在 $U$ 中的补集，即 $\Omega = U \setminus \{P\}$。因此样本空间包含的点数为 $|\Omega| = 64 - 1 = 63$。

2. **计算全集 $U$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值之和**
   - 随机变量 $X(A) = x_1 + x_2 + x_3$。利用求和的线性性质：
     $$\sum_{A \in U} X(A) = \sum_{A \in U} x_1 + \sum_{A \in U} x_2 + \sum_{A \in U} x_3$$
   - 对于坐标 $x_1$，其取值集合为 $\{-2, -1, 1, 2\}$，该集合关于原点对称，元素之和为 $(-2)+(-1)+1+2 = 0$。
   - 在 $U$ 的 64 个点中，$x_1$ 取每一个特定值时，$x_2, x_3$ 各有 4 种选择，共 $4 \times 4 = 16$ 次。因此：
     $$\sum_{A \in U} x_1 = 16 \times [(-2)+(-1)+1+2] = 16 \times 0 = 0$$
   - 由对称性，$\sum_{A \in U} x_2 = 0$，$\sum_{A \in U} x_3 = 0$。
   - 故全集 $U$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值总和为：$\sum_{A \in U} X(A) = 0 + 0 + 0 = 0$。

3. **计算样本空间 $\Omega$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值之和**
   - $\Omega$ 是从 $U$ 中去掉点 $P(1,1,1)$ 得到的。
   - 点 $P$ 对应的 $X$ 值为 $X(P) = 1 + 1 + 1 = 3$。
   - 因此，$\Omega$ 中所有点的 $X$ 值之和为：
     $$\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A) = \sum_{A \in U} X(A) - X(P) = 0 - 3 = -3$$

4. **计算数学期望**
   - 从 $\Omega$ 中等概率随机取一个点，每个点被取到的概率均为 $\frac{1}{63}$。
   - 数学期望定义为所有可能取值乘以其概率之和，即总和除以点数：
     $$E(X) = \frac{\sum_{A \in \Omega} X(A)}{|\Omega|} = \frac{-3}{63} = -\frac{1}{21}$$

5. **对照选项**
   - 计算结果 $-\frac{1}{21}$ 对应选项 A。

9. 设z = 3 + 2i，则（ ）
A. \bar{z} = 3 - 2i
B. |z| = 5
C. z^2 = 5 + 12i
D. \frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
已知 $z = 3 + 2i$，逐项分析如下：

**A选项**：复数的共轭复数定义为 $\bar{z} = a - bi$（$z=a+bi$）。  
代入得 $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$，故 **A正确**。

**B选项**：复数的模公式为 $|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$。  
计算得 $|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13} \neq 5$，故 **B错误**。

**C选项**：计算 $z^2$：  
$z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \times 3 \times 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4(-1) = 5 + 12i$，故 **C正确**。

**D选项**：化简分式 $\frac{z+3}{z-i}$：  
分子：$z + 3 = (3 + 2i) + 3 = 6 + 2i$  
分母：$z - i = (3 + 2i) - i = 3 + i$  
$\frac{z+3}{z-i} = \frac{6+2i}{3+i} = \frac{2(3+i)}{3+i} = 2$  
因为 $2$ 是实数，即 $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$，故 **D正确**。

综上所述，正确选项为 A、C、D。

\boxed{ACD}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process:

1.  **Analyze User Input:**
   - **Question Type:** Multiple-choice question (多选题, meaning more than one option can be correct).
   - **Given:** $z = 3 + 2i$

   - **Task:** Step-by-step reasoning, final answer in `\boxed{}`.

2.  **Evaluate Each Option:**

   *Option A: $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$*
   - Definition of conjugate: If $z = a + bi$, then $\bar{z} = a - bi$.
   - Here, $a = 3$, $b = 2$. So $\bar{z} = 3 - 2i$.
   - Option A is **correct**.

   *Option B: $|z| = 5$*
   - Definition of modulus: $|z| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}$.
   - $|z| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4} = \sqrt{13}$.
   - $\sqrt{13} \neq 5$.
   - Option B is **incorrect**.

   *Option C: $z^2 = 5 + 12i$*
   - Calculate $z^2$: $z^2 = (3 + 2i)^2 = 3^2 + 2 \cdot 3 \cdot 2i + (2i)^2 = 9 + 12i + 4i^2$.
   - Since $i^2 = -1$, $z^2 = 9 + 12i - 4 = 5 + 12i$.
   - Option C is **correct**.

   *Option D: $\frac{z+3}{z-i} \in \mathbf{R}$*
   - Calculate the expression: $\frac{z+3}{z-i}$.
   - Substitute $z = 3 + 2i$:
     Numerator: $z + 3 = (3 + 2i) + 3 = 6 + 2i$.
     Denominator: $z - i = (3 + 2i) - i = 3 + i$.
     Expression: $\frac{6 + 2i}{3 + i}$.
   - Simplify by multiplying numerator and denominator by the conjugate of the denominator $(3 - i)$:
     $\frac{(6 + 2i)(3 - i)}{(3 + i)(3 - i)} = \frac{18 - 6i + 6i - 2i^2}{9 - i^2} = \frac{18 - 2(-1)}{9 - (-1)} = \frac{18 + 2}{10} = \frac{20}{10} = 2$.
   - Since $2 \in \mathbf{R}$, the expression is a real number.
   - Option D is **correct**.

   - All checks pass.

gaokao_000_009 ✓ BC BC 30393 46.7 651.2 http://192.168.0.41:9292
Prompt
请解答以下数学题（多选题）。逐步推理，将最终答案放在 \boxed{} 中。

10. 在空间中，A、B为两个定点，动点C到直线AB的距离为2，动点D到直线AB的距离为1。若二面角C - AB - D为60°，则（ ）
A. \angle CAD \ge 60^\circ
B. CD \ge \sqrt{3}
C. 当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD
D. 当AB \perp平面ACD时，AC \perp AD

请将答案放在 \boxed{} 中。
Response
建立空间直角坐标系，设 $A$ 为原点 $(0,0,0)$，直线 $AB$ 为 $z$ 轴。

根据题意，动点 $C$ 到直线 $AB$ 的距离为 2，动点 $D$ 到直线 $AB$ 的距离为 1，且二面角 $C-AB-D$ 为 $60^\circ$。不妨设：
$C = (2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, z_c)$
$D = (\cos(\theta+60^\circ), \sin(\theta+60^\circ), z_d)$

**1. 分析选项 A：$\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
计算向量 $\vec{AC}$ 与 $\vec{AD}$ 的夹角余弦值：
$\cos \angle CAD = \frac{\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD}}{|\vec{AC}| |\vec{AD}|} = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}}$
取 $z_c = 1, z_d = 1$，则 $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}\sqrt{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{10}}{5} \approx 0.632 > \frac{1}{2}$。
由于余弦值越大，角度越小，故 $\angle CAD < 60^\circ$，选项 A 错误。

**2. 分析选项 B：$CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
点 $C$ 与 $D$ 在 $xy$ 平面上的投影距离平方为：
$d_{xy}^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2 \cdot 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 60^\circ = 5 - 2 = 3$
$CD^2 = d_{xy}^2 + (z_c - z_d)^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2 \ge 3$
故 $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$，选项 B 正确。

**3. 分析选项 C：当 $AB \perp CD$ 时，$CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$**
$AB \perp CD \iff z_c = z_d$。此时 $\vec{CD}$ 为水平向量，且 $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = |D_{xy}|^2 - \vec{D_{xy}} \cdot \vec{C_{xy}} = 1 - 1 = 0$。
由于 $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AB}$ 且 $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$，而 $AB$ 与 $AD$ 是平面 $ABD$ 内相交直线，故 $CD \perp$ 平面 $ABD$，选项 C 正确。

**4. 分析选项 D：当 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$ 时，$AC \perp AD$**
若 $AB \perp$ 平面 $ACD$，则 $AC \perp AB$ 且 $AD \perp AB$，即 $z_c = z_d = 0$。
此时 $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 60^\circ = 1 \ne 0$，即 $AC$ 与 $AD$ 不垂直（夹角为 $60^\circ$），选项 D 错误。

综上所述，正确选项为 B、C。

\boxed{BC}
Reasoning
Here's a thinking process to solve the problem:

1.  **Understand the Goal:** The user wants to solve a multiple-choice geometry problem in 3D space. There are four options (A, B, C, D), and it's possible that more than one is correct.

2.  **Analyze the Given Information:**
    *   **Fixed points:** $A, B$. Let the line passing through them be $L = AB$.
    *   **Moving point $C$:** Distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is 2. Let $C'$ be the projection of $C$ onto $AB$. Then $CC' \perp AB$ and $|CC'| = 2$.
    *   **Moving point $D$:** Distance from $D$ to line $AB$ is 1. Let $D'$ be the projection of $D$ onto $AB$. Then $DD' \perp AB$ and $|DD'| = 1$.
    *   **Dihedral Angle:** The dihedral angle $C - AB - D$ is $60^\circ$.
        *   This means the angle between the plane containing $C$ and perpendicular to $AB$ (let's call it $\alpha$) and the plane containing $D$ and perpendicular to $AB$ (let's call it $\beta$) is $60^\circ$.
        *   Wait, strictly speaking, the dihedral angle is defined between two half-planes intersecting at the edge $AB$.
        *   Usually, if $C$ projects to $C'$ and $D$ projects to $D'$, the dihedral angle is the angle between $CC'$ and $DD'$ *if* $C'$ and $D'$ are the same point. If $C' \neq D'$, the angle between the planes defined by the lines perpendicular to $AB$ at specific points is constant?
        *   Let's re-read the definition. "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$". This usually implies the angle between the plane $ABC$ and the plane $ABD$. Since $CC' \perp AB$ and $DD' \perp AB$, the angle between the planes is the angle between the lines $CC'$ and $DD'$ *after* they are translated to the same intersection point on the line $AB$.
        *   Let's set up a coordinate system to be precise.

3.  **Coordinate System Setup:**
    *   Let line $AB$ be the $z$-axis.
    *   Let point $A$ be the origin $(0,0,0)$. (Since $A$ is just a fixed point, this simplifies things, but $B$ is also fixed. Let $B = (0,0,b)$. Actually, the distance constraints are relative to the line $AB$, so the position of $A$ and $B$ on the line matters for specific lengths like $AC$, but maybe not for general geometric relationships. Let's keep $A$ at origin for now).
    *   Let $A = (0,0,0)$.
    *   Line $AB$ is the $z$-axis.
    *   Point $C = (x_c, y_c, z_c)$.
    *   Distance from $C$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{x_c^2 + y_c^2} = 2$.
    *   Point $D = (x_d, y_d, z_d)$.
    *   Distance from $D$ to $z$-axis is $\sqrt{x_d^2 + y_d^2} = 1$.
    *   **Dihedral Angle Condition:** The angle between the plane passing through $C$ and $AB$ (which contains vector $\vec{AC}$ or just the projection vector) and the plane passing through $D$ and $AB$ is $60^\circ$.
    *   In cylindrical coordinates (or polar coordinates in $xy$-plane):
        *   $C = (2 \cos \theta_c, 2 \sin \theta_c, z_c)$.
        *   $D = (1 \cos \theta_d, 1 \sin \theta_d, z_d)$.
        *   The angle between the planes is $|\theta_c - \theta_d| = 60^\circ$ (or $300^\circ$, etc., but usually taken as acute/obtuse angle between planes). The problem says "dihedral angle ... is $60^\circ$". So $|\theta_c - \theta_d| = 60^\circ$. Let's assume $\theta_c = 60^\circ$ and $\theta_d = 0^\circ$ without loss of generality (rotating around $z$-axis).
    *   So, coordinates:
        *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
        *   $D = (1, 0, z_d)$. (Since distance to $z$-axis is 1, we can align $D$ on the $xz$-plane).
        *   $C = (2 \cos 60^\circ, 2 \sin 60^\circ, z_c) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.

4.  **Analyze Each Option:**

    **Option A: $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$**
    *   We need to calculate the angle $\angle CAD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1\cdot1 + \sqrt{3}\cdot0 + z_c z_d = 1 + z_c z_d$.
    *   Magnitudes:
        *   $|\vec{AC}| = \sqrt{1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 + z_c^2} = \sqrt{4 + z_c^2}$.
        *   $|\vec{AD}| = \sqrt{1^2 + 0^2 + z_d^2} = \sqrt{1 + z_d^2}$.
    *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}}$.
    *   We want to check if $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$, which is equivalent to $\cos(\angle CAD) \le \cos(60^\circ) = 1/2$.
    *   Inequality to check: $\frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$?
    *   Square both sides? Wait, $1 + z_c z_d$ can be negative. If it's negative, angle is $> 90^\circ$, so condition holds. If positive, we check.
    *   Is it always true?
    *   Let's try to minimize the cosine (maximize the angle) or check if it can be greater than $1/2$.
    *   We need to find if there exist $z_c, z_d$ such that $\cos(\angle CAD) > 1/2$.
    *   Let $z_c = 0, z_d = 0$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   Dot product = 1.
        *   $|\vec{AC}| = 2$, $|\vec{AD}| = 1$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1}{2 \cdot 1} = 1/2$.
        *   $\theta = 60^\circ$.
    *   Let's try $z_c$ large positive, $z_d$ large positive?
    *   Actually, consider the geometry.
    *   $C$ projects to $C'(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$ in $xy$ plane? No, $C'$ is projection onto $AB$ ($z$-axis). So $C' = (0,0,z_c)$.
    *   Wait, in the setup $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$, the projection onto $z$-axis is $(0,0,z_c)$. Distance to axis is $\sqrt{1+3} = 2$. Correct.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z_d)$, projection is $(0,0,z_d)$. Distance is 1. Correct.
    *   The vectors are $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$.
    *   Note that $\vec{AC} = \vec{AC'} + \vec{C'C}$ where $\vec{AC'} = (0,0,z_c)$ and $\vec{C'C} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Actually $\vec{C'C} = C - C' = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$. Length 2.
    *   $\vec{AD} = \vec{AD'} + \vec{D'D}$ where $\vec{AD'} = (0,0,z_d)$ and $\vec{D'D} = (1, 0, 0)$. Length 1.
    *   Wait, $A$ is origin $(0,0,0)$. $C'$ is $(0,0,z_c)$? No, $C'$ is projection of $C$ on $AB$. Yes, $(0,0,z_c)$.
    *   But $A$ is on the line $AB$. So $A$ is on the $z$-axis.
    *   So $\vec{AC}$ connects $(0,0,0)$ to $(1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
    *   $\vec{AD}$ connects $(0,0,0)$ to $(1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   Let's look at the "shadow" on the plane perpendicular to $AB$ (the $xy$-plane).
    *   Projection of $C$ is $P_C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$. Length $AP_C = 2$.
    *   Projection of $D$ is $P_D = (1, 0, 0)$. Length $AP_D = 1$.
    *   Angle $\angle P_C A P_D$ is the angle between the planes, which is $60^\circ$.
    *   In $\triangle P_C A P_D$ (in $xy$-plane), sides are $AP_C=2$, $AP_D=1$, angle $60^\circ$.
    *   By Cosine Rule on $\triangle P_C A P_D$: $|P_C P_D|^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos(60^\circ) = 4 + 1 - 2 = 3$. So distance between projections is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Now consider the actual points in 3D.
    *   $\angle CAD$ is the angle between vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$.
    *   Let $\alpha$ be the angle between $\vec{AC}$ and $AB$ (z-axis), and $\beta$ be angle between $\vec{AD}$ and $AB$.
    *   This seems complicated. Let's go back to the dot product formula.
    *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}}$.
    *   We want to know if $\cos(\angle CAD) \le 1/2$ always?
    *   Let's check if $\frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}}$ can be greater than $1/2$.
    *   Try $z_c = 0, z_d = 0 \implies \cos = 1/2$.
    *   Try $z_c = -1, z_d = -1$.
        *   Numerator: $1 + (-1)(-1) = 2$.
        *   Denominator: $\sqrt{4+1}\sqrt{1+1} = \sqrt{5}\sqrt{2} = \sqrt{10} \approx 3.16$.
        *   Ratio: $2 / 3.16 \approx 0.63 > 0.5$.
        *   Wait, if $\cos > 0.5$, then angle $< 60^\circ$.
        *   So $\angle CAD$ can be less than $60^\circ$.
    *   Let's verify this specific case geometrically.
        *   $A=(0,0,0)$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, -1)$. Distance to $z$-axis $\sqrt{1+3}=2$. OK.
        *   $D = (1, 0, -1)$. Distance to $z$-axis $\sqrt{1+0}=1$. OK.
        *   Angle between planes? $C$ projects to $(1, \sqrt{3})$, angle $60^\circ$. $D$ projects to $(1, 0)$, angle $0^\circ$. Difference $60^\circ$. OK.
        *   Vector $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, -1)$. Length $\sqrt{1+3+1} = \sqrt{5}$.
        *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, -1)$. Length $\sqrt{1+0+1} = \sqrt{2}$.
        *   Dot product: $1(1) + \sqrt{3}(0) + (-1)(-1) = 1 + 1 = 2$.
        *   $\cos \theta = \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}\sqrt{2}} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{10}} = \frac{\sqrt{10}}{5} \approx \frac{3.16}{5} = 0.632$.
        *   $\cos 60^\circ = 0.5$.
        *   Since $0.632 > 0.5$, angle is acute and smaller than $60^\circ$.
    *   **Conclusion for A:** Option A claims $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$. We found a counterexample where $\angle CAD \approx 50.7^\circ$. So **A is False**.

    **Option B: $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$**
    *   Calculate distance $CD$.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (1-1)^2 + (\sqrt{3}-0)^2 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
    *   $CD^2 = 0 + 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
    *   Since $(z_c - z_d)^2 \ge 0$, we have $CD^2 \ge 3$.
    *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Equality holds when $z_c = z_d$.
    *   Is there any constraint preventing $z_c = z_d$?
        *   $C$ is a moving point, $D$ is a moving point. $z_c, z_d$ can be anything.
        *   Wait, are there other constraints? "A, B fixed points". This just defines the line.
        *   "Distance to line AB is 2/1".
        *   "Dihedral angle 60".
        *   Nothing restricts $z_c$ and $z_d$ relative to each other, other than the definitions.
    *   Wait, is it possible that the dihedral angle definition restricts the $z$ coordinates? No, dihedral angle is between planes. The planes are defined by the lines perpendicular to the edge at any point?
    *   Actually, strictly speaking, a dihedral angle is defined by two half-planes originating from a line. For any point on the line, the cross section angle is constant. So the angle between vectors $\vec{C'C}$ and $\vec{D'D}$ (where $C', D'$ are projections on line) is $60^\circ$.
    *   In our coordinates, $\vec{C'C} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$ and $\vec{D'D} = (1, 0, 0)$. The angle between these vectors is indeed $60^\circ$.
    *   So $z_c$ and $z_d$ are independent.
    *   Therefore, minimum distance occurs when $z_c = z_d$, giving $CD = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   **Conclusion for B:** Option B is **True**.

    **Option C: When $AB \perp CD$, then $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB}$ is along $z$-axis, direction $(0,0,1)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = D - C = (1-1, 0-\sqrt{3}, z_d-z_c) = (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_d-z_c)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = 0(0) + 0(-\sqrt{3}) + 1(z_d-z_c) = z_d - z_c$.
    *   For $AB \perp CD$, we need $z_d - z_c = 0$, i.e., $z_c = z_d$.
    *   So the condition $AB \perp CD$ implies that $C$ and $D$ have the same $z$-coordinate (same projection on line $AB$).
    *   Let's check the conclusion: $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains points $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   Since $A$ and $B$ are on the $z$-axis, the plane $ABD$ is determined by the $z$-axis and point $D$.
    *   $D$ is at $(1, 0, z_d)$. The vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   The plane $ABD$ contains the $z$-axis and the point $(1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   Wait, any plane containing the $z$-axis is of the form $y = kx$.
    *   Since $D$ has coordinates $(1, 0, z_d)$, the $y$-coordinate is 0. So the plane $ABD$ is the plane $y=0$ (the $xz$-plane).
    *   Let's verify. $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,b)$, $D=(1,0,z_d)$. All have $y=0$. So yes, Plane $ABD$ is $y=0$.
    *   Now consider vector $\vec{CD}$.
    *   Under the condition $z_c = z_d$, $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   This vector is parallel to the $y$-axis.
    *   The plane $ABD$ is the $xz$-plane ($y=0$).
    *   A vector parallel to the $y$-axis is perpendicular to the $xz$-plane.
    *   So $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   **Conclusion for C:** Option C is **True**.

    **Option D: When $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$, then $AC \perp AD$.**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.
    *   $AB$ is the $z$-axis.
    *   If the $z$-axis is perpendicular to plane $ACD$, then plane $ACD$ must be perpendicular to the $z$-axis.
    *   This means plane $ACD$ is a horizontal plane (constant $z$).
    *   This implies that points $A, C, D$ must have the same $z$-coordinate.
    *   $A$ is at $(0,0,0)$. So $z_A = 0$.
    *   Therefore, we must have $z_c = 0$ and $z_d = 0$.
    *   Let's check if this is possible.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$. Distance to $z$-axis is 2. OK.
        *   $D = (1, 0, 0)$. Distance to $z$-axis is 1. OK.
        *   Plane $ACD$ passes through $(0,0,0)$, $(1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$, $(1, 0, 0)$.
        *   These points are all in the $z=0$ plane ($xy$-plane).
        *   The $z$-axis ($AB$) is perpendicular to the $xy$-plane. So the condition holds.
    *   Now check the conclusion: $AC \perp AD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1(1) + \sqrt{3}(0) + 0(0) = 1$.
    *   Is the dot product 0? No, it's 1.
    *   So $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   Angle is $60^\circ$ (as calculated in Option A with $z=0$).
    *   **Conclusion for D:** Option D is **False**.

5.  **Review and Double Check:**

    *   **Setup:**
        *   Line $AB$ is $z$-axis.
        *   $A$ is origin.
        *   $C$ projects to circle of radius 2 in $xy$ plane? No, $C$ is distance 2 from line. So $x^2+y^2=4$.
        *   $D$ projects to circle of radius 1 in $xy$ plane? So $x^2+y^2=1$.
        *   Dihedral angle $60^\circ$ means angle between plane $(ABC)$ and $(ABD)$ is $60^\circ$.
        *   Plane $(ABC)$ contains $C$ and $z$-axis.
        *   Plane $(ABD)$ contains $D$ and $z$-axis.
        *   In $xy$ projection, these are lines from origin. Angle between lines is $60^\circ$.
        *   Let $D$ be on positive $x$-axis in projection. $D = (1, 0, z_d)$.
        *   Then $C$ is at angle $60^\circ$. $C = (2 \cos 60, 2 \sin 60, z_c) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
        *   This setup seems robust.

    *   **Re-evaluating Option A:**
        *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
        *   We found a case $z_c=-1, z_d=-1$ giving $\cos \approx 0.63 > 0.5$, so angle $< 60^\circ$.
        *   Are there constraints on $z_c, z_d$? No.
        *   Wait, is it possible that $A$ and $B$ being fixed points constrains something?
        *   $A$ is fixed, $B$ is fixed. $AB$ is a segment.
        *   $C$ and $D$ are moving points. The distances are to the *line* $AB$. Usually, "distance to line AB" means the infinite line. If it meant segment, it would say segment. Standard interpretation is infinite line.
        *   Even if it were segment, as long as the segment is long enough or $z$ range covers 0, the counterexample holds. $A$ is at $z=0$. If $B$ is at $z=10$, $z_c=-1$ is allowed (projection on line is outside segment, but distance to line is defined).
        *   Wait, usually distance to a line segment is distance to the closest point on the segment. But the text says "distance to line AB" (直线AB). In Chinese math problems, "直线AB" means the infinite line passing through A and B. "线段AB" is the segment. So infinite line is correct.
        *   Is there any geometric reason $\angle CAD$ must be $\ge 60$?
        *   Let's visualize. $C$ and $D$ are on cones around the axis $AB$. Cone for $C$ has semi-vertical angle $\alpha$ where $\tan \alpha = 2/z_c$? No.
        *   Let $\phi_C$ be angle of $AC$ with axis. $\tan \phi_C = 2/|z_c|$.
        *   Let $\phi_D$ be angle of $AD$ with axis. $\tan \phi_D = 1/|z_d|$.
        *   The angle between vectors is determined by spherical geometry?
        *   Imagine a sphere centered at A. $C$ and $D$ intersect the sphere at points $C_0, D_0$.
        *   The distance of $C$ from axis is $AC \sin \phi_C = 2$. So $AC = 2 / \sin \phi_C$.
        *   The distance of $D$ from axis is $AD \sin \phi_D = 1$. So $AD = 1 / \sin \phi_D$.
        *   The angle between the planes is the difference in longitude, $\Delta \lambda = 60^\circ$.
        *   The angle $\angle CAD$ is the central angle between $C_0$ and $D_0$ on the sphere.
        *   Spherical law of cosines:
            $\cos(\angle CAD) = \cos \phi_C \cos \phi_D + \sin \phi_C \sin \phi_D \cos(60^\circ)$.
            $\cos(\angle CAD) = \cos \phi_C \cos \phi_D + \frac{1}{2} \sin \phi_C \sin \phi_D$.
        *   We want to check if $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$, i.e., $\cos(\angle CAD) \le 1/2$.
        *   Is $\cos \phi_C \cos \phi_D + \frac{1}{2} \sin \phi_C \sin \phi_D \le \frac{1}{2}$ always?
        *   Let's check.
        *   Rearrange: $\frac{1}{2} \le \cos \phi_C \cos \phi_D + \frac{1}{2} \sin \phi_C \sin \phi_D$? No, we check if $\le 1/2$.
        *   Actually, if we pick $\phi_C, \phi_D$ close to 0 (points far away on axis), $\cos \approx 1, \sin \approx 0$.
        *   Then $\cos(\angle CAD) \approx 1$. Angle $\approx 0$.
        *   Wait, is it possible to have $\phi$ close to 0?
        *   $C$ is distance 2 from axis. $D$ is distance 1.
        *   If $C$ is very far from $A$ along the line (large $z_c$), the angle $\phi_C$ is small.
        *   Example: $z_c = 100, z_d = 100$.
        *   $C \approx (0,0,100)$, $D \approx (0,0,100)$. Distance from axis is small relative to $z$.
        *   Angle $CAD$ will be very small.
        *   Let's check the formula:
            $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + 10000}{\sqrt{4+10000}\sqrt{1+10000}} \approx \frac{10000}{100 \cdot 100} = 1$.
            So $\theta \approx 0$.
        *   $0 < 60$. So A is definitely false.
        *   Why did I doubt? Maybe confusion with projection angle. The projection angle is fixed at 60, but the 3D angle between vectors from origin can be anything depending on $z$.
        *   Wait, does "distance to line AB" constrain $z$?
        *   Distance is perpendicular distance. For a point $(x,y,z)$, distance is $\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$. This is independent of $z$.
        *   So yes, $C$ can be arbitrarily far "up" the line.
        *   So Option A is clearly false.

    *   **Re-evaluating Option B:**
        *   $CD^2 = (x_c-x_d)^2 + (y_c-y_d)^2 + (z_c-z_d)^2$.
        *   $(x_c, y_c)$ is vector of length 2 at angle $\theta_c$.
        *   $(x_d, y_d)$ is vector of length 1 at angle $\theta_d$.
        *   $|\theta_c - \theta_d| = 60^\circ$.
        *   Distance squared in $xy$-plane (projection distance): $d_{xy}^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos(60^\circ) = 5 - 2 = 3$.
        *   $CD^2 = d_{xy}^2 + (z_c - z_d)^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
        *   Since $(z_c - z_d)^2 \ge 0$, $CD^2 \ge 3 \implies CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
        *   This seems solid.
        *   Is there any catch? "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$".
        *   Does this imply $C$ and $D$ are on specific sides?
        *   Dihedral angle is usually in $[0, 180]$. Given as 60.
        *   This fixes the relative angular position.
        *   So yes, B is correct.

    *   **Re-evaluating Option C:**
        *   Condition $AB \perp CD$.
        *   $AB$ is vector $\vec{k} = (0,0,1)$.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
        *   $D = (1, 0, z_d)$.
        *   $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, z_d - z_c)$.
        *   Dot product with $\vec{k}$: $z_d - z_c$.
        *   Condition implies $z_c = z_d$.
        *   Then $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   Plane $ABD$. Points $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(1,0,z_d)$.
        *   Vectors in plane: $\vec{AB} = (0,0,b)$ (along z-axis), $\vec{AD} = (1,0,z_d)$.
        *   Normal vector to plane $ABD$: $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD}$.
        *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$ (direction).
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1,0,z_d)$.
        *   $\vec{n} = \det \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 & z_d \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i}(0) - \mathbf{j}(-1) + \mathbf{k}(0) = (0, 1, 0)$.
        *   Normal is along $y$-axis.
        *   Vector $\vec{CD}$ is $(0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$, which is along $y$-axis.
        *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal $\vec{n}$.
        *   So $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
        *   This holds.
        *   Wait, is $B$ distinct from $A$? Yes, "two fixed points".
        *   Is $D$ distinct from line $AB$? Distance is 1, so yes.
        *   Is plane $ABD$ well defined? Yes.
        *   Is $CD$ non-zero? Yes.
        *   So C is correct.

    *   **Re-evaluating Option D:**
        *   Condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.
        *   $AB$ is $z$-axis. Plane $ACD$ must be perpendicular to $z$-axis.
        *   So plane $ACD$ is $z = \text{const}$.
        *   Since $A(0,0,0)$ is in the plane, $z=0$.
        *   So $C$ and $D$ must have $z$-coordinate 0.
        *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   Check $AC \perp AD$.
        *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
        *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
        *   Dot product $1 \ne 0$.
        *   So $AC$ not perpendicular to $AD$.
        *   Is it possible $A$ is not origin?
        *   Coordinate system choice is arbitrary but geometry is invariant.
        *   If $A$ is origin, condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$ means vector $\vec{AB}$ is normal to plane $ACD$.
        *   Since $A$ is in plane, vector $\vec{AB}$ is normal to any vector in plane passing through $A$.
        *   So $\vec{AB} \perp \vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AB} \perp \vec{AD}$.
        *   This means $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$.
        *   Since distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is 2, and $AC \perp AB$, then $AC$ must be the perpendicular segment?
        *   Distance from point to line is the length of the perpendicular segment.
        *   If $AC \perp AB$, then $AC$ *is* the perpendicular segment (assuming $A$ is the projection).
        *   Wait, $A$ is a point on the line.
        *   If $AC \perp AB$, then the distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is exactly length $AC$.
        *   So $|AC| = 2$.
        *   Similarly, if $AD \perp AB$, then distance from $D$ to line $AB$ is length $AD$.
        *   So $|AD| = 1$.
        *   We are given distance $C$ to line is 2, $D$ to line is 1.
        *   So the condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$ implies $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$, which is consistent with distances being lengths $AC$ and $AD$ only if $A$ is the foot of the perpendiculars.
        *   Wait, does $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$ imply $AC \perp AB$?
        *   Yes, if a line is perpendicular to a plane, it is perpendicular to every line in the plane passing through the intersection point.
        *   Intersection of $AB$ and plane $ACD$ is $A$.
        *   So $AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
        *   This means the projection of $C$ onto line $AB$ is $A$.
        *   Distance from $C$ to line $AB$ is $|CA| = 2$.
        *   Projection of $D$ onto line $AB$ is $A$.
        *   Distance from $D$ to line $AB$ is $|DA| = 1$.
        *   Now consider the angle $\angle CAD$.
        *   $C$ is in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$. $D$ is in the same plane.
        *   The angle between them in that plane is determined by the dihedral angle.
        *   The dihedral angle is the angle between the plane $ABC$ (which is plane $AB C_{proj}$) and $ABD$.
        *   Wait, if $C$ projects to $A$, then plane $ABC$ is the plane containing $AB$ and $AC$. Since $AC \perp AB$, this is well defined.
        *   Similarly for $D$.
        *   The angle between these two planes is the angle between $AC$ and $AD$.
        *   We are given dihedral angle is $60^\circ$.
        *   Therefore, $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
        *   Is $AC \perp AD$?
        *   That would require $\angle CAD = 90^\circ$.
        *   But $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
        *   So $AC$ is not perpendicular to $AD$.
        *   So Option D is False.
        *   Let's check if there's any ambiguity.
        *   "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$".
        *   Planes are $ABC$ and $ABD$.
        *   If $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$, then the angle between the planes is exactly the angle between $AC$ and $AD$.
        *   Given as 60.
        *   So angle is 60. Not 90.
        *   Thus $AC \not\perp AD$.
        *   Option D is definitely false.

6.  **Synthesizing the Result:**
    *   A: False (Counterexample $z_c, z_d$ large or specific negative values).
    *   B: True (Distance calculation).
    *   C: True (Vector analysis).
    *   D: False (Geometry of perpendicularity).

    *   Wait, let me double check Option A's counterexample carefully.
    *   We need $\cos(\angle CAD) > 1/2$.
    *   $\frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4 + z_c^2}\sqrt{1 + z_d^2}} > \frac{1}{2}$.
    *   Let $z_c = 1, z_d = 1$.
    *   Num: 2. Denom: $\sqrt{5}\sqrt{2} = \sqrt{10} \approx 3.16$.
    *   Ratio $\approx 0.63 > 0.5$.
    *   So $\angle CAD < 60^\circ$.
    *   Is this configuration valid?
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 1)$. Dist to $z$-axis $\sqrt{1+3} = 2$. Correct.
    *   $D = (1, 0, 1)$. Dist to $z$-axis $\sqrt{1} = 1$. Correct.
    *   Angle between planes: $C$ is at angle 60 in $xy$, $D$ at 0. Angle is 60. Correct.
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 1)$, $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 1)$.
    *   Dot prod: $1+0+1 = 2$.
    *   $|\vec{AC}| = \sqrt{1+3+1} = \sqrt{5}$.
    *   $|\vec{AD}| = \sqrt{1+0+1} = \sqrt{2}$.
    *   $\cos = 2 / \sqrt{10} = \sqrt{10}/5 \approx 0.632$.
    *   $\arccos(0.632) \approx 50.8^\circ$.
    *   $50.8 < 60$.
    *   So A is False.

    *   Wait, is "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$" defined as the angle between vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$?
    *   No. Dihedral angle is angle between planes.
    *   Is it possible that the dihedral angle is defined by the half-planes starting at $AB$?
    *   Yes.
    *   Is it possible that the points $C$ and $D$ must be on the same side of the plane passing through $AB$ perpendicular to something? No.
    *   Usually, dihedral angle is a non-directed angle between planes, or an angle in $[0, \pi]$.
    *   So my interpretation holds.

    *   Let's check Option B again.
    *   $CD^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
    *   Is it possible for $CD$ to be smaller?
    *   The distance between two lines parallel to $z$-axis? No, $C$ and $D$ are points.
    *   $C$ is on cylinder $x^2+y^2=4$.
    *   $D$ is on cylinder $x^2+y^2=1$.
    *   The angle in polar coordinates differs by $60^\circ$.
    *   Let $C = (2 \cos \theta, 2 \sin \theta, z_c)$.
    *   Let $D = (1 \cos(\theta + 60^\circ), 1 \sin(\theta + 60^\circ), z_d)$.
    *   $CD^2 = (2\cos\theta - \cos(\theta+60))^2 + (2\sin\theta - \sin(\theta+60))^2 + (z_c-z_d)^2$.
    *   Let $\alpha = \theta, \beta = \theta + 60$.
    *   $x$-diff: $2\cos\alpha - \cos\beta$.
    *   $y$-diff: $2\sin\alpha - \sin\beta$.
    *   Sum of squares: $4\cos^2\alpha + \cos^2\beta - 4\cos\alpha\cos\beta + 4\sin^2\alpha + \sin^2\beta - 4\sin\alpha\sin\beta$.
    *   $= 4(\cos^2+\sin^2) + (\cos^2+\sin^2) - 4(\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta)$.
    *   $= 4 + 1 - 4\cos(\alpha - \beta)$.
    *   $\alpha - \beta = -60^\circ$. $\cos(-60) = 1/2$.
    *   $= 5 - 4(1/2) = 3$.
    *   So horizontal distance squared is always 3.
    *   So $CD^2 = 3 + (z_c-z_d)^2 \ge 3$.
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Correct.

    *   Let's check Option C again.
    *   Condition: $AB \perp CD$.
    *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$ (direction).
    *   $\vec{CD} = D - C$.
    *   $C = (2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, z_c)$.
    *   $D = (\cos(\theta+60), \sin(\theta+60), z_d)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (\cos(\theta+60) - 2\cos\theta, \sin(\theta+60) - 2\sin\theta, z_d - z_c)$.
    *   Dot product with $(0,0,1)$ is $z_d - z_c$.
    *   So $AB \perp CD \implies z_c = z_d$.
    *   Conclusion: $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ passes through $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,b)$, $D(\dots)$.
    *   Since $A, B$ on $z$-axis, plane contains $z$-axis.
    *   Normal to plane $ABD$ is perpendicular to $z$-axis.
    *   Vector $\vec{AD}$ is in plane. $\vec{AD} = D - A = D$.
    *   $D = (x_d, y_d, z_d)$.
    *   Since plane contains $z$-axis, its normal must be perpendicular to $z$-axis, so $n_z = 0$.
    *   Also normal is perpendicular to $\vec{AD}$.
    *   Wait, if plane contains $z$-axis, it is defined by the angle of the radial vector of any point in it (excluding axis).
    *   $D$ is in plane. So plane is the plane containing $z$-axis and point $D$.
    *   Normal vector $\vec{n}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{k}=(0,0,1)$ and $\vec{D}=(x_d, y_d, z_d)$.
    *   $\vec{n} = \vec{k} \times \vec{D} = (0,0,1) \times (x_d, y_d, z_d) = (-y_d, x_d, 0)$.
    *   So $\vec{n} = (-y_d, x_d, 0)$.
    *   We need to check if $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to $\vec{n}$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = D - C = (x_d - x_c, y_d - y_c, 0)$ (since $z_c=z_d$).
    *   We need to check if $(x_d - x_c, y_d - y_c, 0)$ is parallel to $(-y_d, x_d, 0)$.
    *   Or simply, is $\vec{CD}$ perpendicular to the plane?
    *   Plane is spanned by $\vec{k}$ and $\vec{D}$.
    *   We know $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{k} = 0$ (from condition $z_c=z_d$).
    *   We need to check if $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{D} = 0$? No, that would mean $CD \perp AD$.
    *   We need to check if $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to the plane.
    *   This means $\vec{CD}$ must be perpendicular to two independent vectors in the plane.
    *   $\vec{k}$ is one. We already have $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{k}$.
    *   We need $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$? No.
    *   Wait, $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$ means $CD$ is normal to the plane.
    *   The plane contains $AB$ (z-axis) and $D$.
    *   If $CD \perp AB$ (which is given), $CD$ is horizontal.
    *   For $CD$ to be normal to the plane containing $AB$ and $D$, $CD$ must be perpendicular to $D$'s projection?
    *   Let's visualize.
    *   Look from top (xy plane).
    *   $AB$ is a point $O$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ appears as a line $OD$ passing through origin.
    *   $C$ is a point $C_{xy}$. $D$ is a point $D_{xy}$.
    *   Vector $CD$ in 3D projects to vector $C_{xy}D_{xy}$ in 2D.
    *   For $CD$ to be perpendicular to plane $ABD$, the vector $CD$ must be perpendicular to the line $OD$ in the projection?
    *   Wait. The plane $ABD$ is vertical. It is the plane containing the $z$-axis and the vector $\vec{OD_{xy}}$? No, $\vec{OD}$. But projection is $\vec{OD_{xy}}$.
    *   Actually, the plane $ABD$ is the plane passing through $z$-axis and $D$. In projection, it looks like the line connecting origin to $D_{xy}$.
    *   The normal to the plane $ABD$ is a horizontal vector perpendicular to the projection of $D$ (vector $\vec{OD_{xy}}$).
    *   Wait, normal is $(-y_d, x_d, 0)$. This is perpendicular to $(x_d, y_d)$.
    *   Yes.
    *   So we need $\vec{CD}$ to be parallel to $(-y_d, x_d, 0)$.
    *   We know $\vec{CD} = (x_d - x_c, y_d - y_c, 0)$.
    *   So we need vector $\vec{C_{xy}D_{xy}}$ to be perpendicular to $\vec{OD_{xy}}$?
    *   No. We need $\vec{C_{xy}D_{xy}}$ to be parallel to the normal of the plane.
    *   The normal of the plane is perpendicular to the plane. The plane contains $\vec{OD_{xy}}$ (and z-axis).
    *   So normal is perpendicular to $\vec{OD_{xy}}$.
    *   So yes, we need $\vec{C_{xy}D_{xy}} \perp \vec{OD_{xy}}$.
    *   Let's check this dot product: $\vec{CD_{xy}} \cdot \vec{OD_{xy}} = 0$?
    *   $\vec{CD_{xy}} = D_{xy} - C_{xy}$.
    *   $\vec{OD_{xy}} = D_{xy}$.
    *   Dot product: $(D_{xy} - C_{xy}) \cdot D_{xy} = |D_{xy}|^2 - C_{xy} \cdot D_{xy}$.
    *   We know $|D_{xy}| = 1$ (distance to line). So $|D_{xy}|^2 = 1$.
    *   We know $|C_{xy}| = 2$.
    *   Angle between $C_{xy}$ and $D_{xy}$ is $60^\circ$.
    *   So $C_{xy} \cdot D_{xy} = |C_{xy}| |D_{xy}| \cos(60^\circ) = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot 0.5 = 1$.
    *   So dot product = $1 - 1 = 0$.
    *   Yes!
    *   So $\vec{CD_{xy}}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{OD_{xy}}$.
    *   Since $\vec{CD}$ is horizontal (z-diff 0), $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to the normal of the plane? No.
    *   Wait.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and $D_{xy}$.
    *   Normal to plane is vector $\vec{n}$ in $xy$ plane perpendicular to $D_{xy}$.
    *   We found $\vec{CD_{xy}}$ is perpendicular to $D_{xy}$.
    *   So $\vec{CD_{xy}}$ is parallel to $\vec{n}$.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is parallel to normal of plane $ABD$.
    *   So $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Logic holds.
    *   Let's re-verify the dot product calculation.
    *   $C_{xy}$ vector at angle 60, length 2. $C_{xy} = (1, \sqrt{3})$.
    *   $D_{xy}$ vector at angle 0, length 1. $D_{xy} = (1, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{CD_{xy}} = D_{xy} - C_{xy} = (0, -\sqrt{3})$.
    *   $\vec{OD_{xy}} = (1, 0)$.
    *   Dot product: $0\cdot1 + (-\sqrt{3})\cdot0 = 0$.
    *   Yes, they are perpendicular.
    *   Wait, if $\vec{CD_{xy}} \perp \vec{OD_{xy}}$, then $CD$ is perpendicular to the line in the plane $ABD$ that is horizontal?
    *   The plane $ABD$ contains the $z$-axis (vertical) and the line $AD$ (slanted).
    *   Actually, plane $ABD$ contains the vector $\vec{OD_{xy}}$ (horizontal part of $AD$).
    *   If $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{OD_{xy}}$ (horizontal) and $\vec{CD}$ is horizontal (perpendicular to $z$-axis), then $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to two non-parallel vectors in the plane?
    *   Vectors in plane $ABD$: $\vec{k} = (0,0,1)$ and $\vec{v} = (x_d, y_d, z_d)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (x_d-x_c, y_d-y_c, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{k} = 0$ (satisfied by $z_c=z_d$).
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{v}$?
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{v} = (x_d-x_c)x_d + (y_d-y_c)y_d + 0\cdot z_d$.
    *   $= x_d^2 + y_d^2 - (x_c x_d + y_c y_d)$.
    *   $x_d^2 + y_d^2 = |D_{xy}|^2 = 1$.
    *   $x_c x_d + y_c y_d = C_{xy} \cdot D_{xy} = 1$.
    *   So dot product is $1 - 1 = 0$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{v}$ (vector $AD$).
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{k}$ (vector $AB$) and $\vec{v}$ (vector $AD$).
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to the plane spanned by $AB$ and $AD$, which is plane $ABD$.
    *   Yes. Option C is correct.

    *   **Re-evaluating Option D:**
    *   Condition: $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.
    *   $AB$ is $z$-axis. Plane $ACD$ is perpendicular to $z$-axis.
    *   Plane $ACD$ contains $A(0,0,0)$, so it must be the plane $z=0$ (xy-plane).
    *   So $C$ and $D$ must have $z$-coordinate 0.
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Conclusion: $AC \perp AD$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Dot product $1 \ne 0$.
    *   Angle is 60 degrees.
    *   Is there any other interpretation?
    *   Maybe $A$ is not origin?
    *   Let $A = (0,0,a)$. $B = (0,0,b)$.
    *   Line $AB$ is still $z$-axis.
    *   Condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB} = (0,0, b-a)$ is normal to plane $ACD$.
    *   So plane $ACD$ is horizontal ($z = \text{const}$).
    *   Since $A$ is in plane, plane is $z=a$.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ must have $z$-coordinate $a$.
    *   Let's shift coordinates so $A$ is origin. Same result.
    *   Wait, maybe $C$ and $D$ are not at $z=a$?
    *   If plane is $z=a$, all points in it must have $z=a$.
    *   $A$ is $(0,0,a)$.
    *   $C$ must be $(x_c, y_c, a)$.
    *   Distance to line $AB$ (z-axis) is $\sqrt{x_c^2+y_c^2} = 2$.
    *   $D$ must be $(x_d, y_d, a)$.
    *   Distance to line $AB$ is $\sqrt{x_d^2+y_d^2} = 1$.
    *   Dihedral angle 60.
    *   Angle between vectors $(x_c, y_c)$ and $(x_d, y_d)$ is 60.
    *   Vectors $\vec{AC} = (x_c, y_c, 0)$ (relative to A).
    *   Vector $\vec{AD} = (x_d, y_d, 0)$ (relative to A).
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = x_c x_d + y_c y_d = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 60 = 1$.
    *   Magnitudes $|\vec{AC}|=2, |\vec{AD}|=1$.
    *   $\cos(\angle CAD) = 1 / (2\cdot 1) = 1/2$.
    *   Angle is 60.
    *   Not 90.
    *   So $AC$ not perpendicular to $AD$.
    *   D is false.

    *   **Wait, let me check the question text again carefully.**
    *   "10. 在空间中，A、B为两个定点，动点C到直线AB的距离为2，动点D到直线AB的距离为1。若二面角C - AB - D为60°，则（ ）"
    *   Standard definitions apply.
    *   "A. $\angle CAD \ge 60^\circ$" - Checked, False.
    *   "B. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$" - Checked, True.
    *   "C. 当AB \perp CD时，CD \perp平面ABD" - Checked, True.
    *   "D. 当AB \perp平面ACD时，AC \perp AD" - Checked, False.

    *   **Double Check Option A counterexample validity.**
    *   Maybe there's a constraint I missed.
    *   "A, B fixed points".
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ must project onto the segment $AB$?
    *   "Distance to line AB" (直线) vs "Distance to segment AB" (线段).
    *   Usually "直线" means infinite line.
    *   If it were segment, distance would be min dist to endpoints if projection falls outside.
    *   But it explicitly says "直线AB".
    *   Also "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$".
    *   Dihedral angle is defined for the whole planes.
    *   If $C$ and $D$ are far away, the planes $ABC$ and $ABD$ are well defined.
    *   Is it possible that the angle $\angle CAD$ has a lower bound?
    *   Let's check the spherical geometry formula again.
    *   $\cos \theta = \cos \phi_C \cos \phi_D + \sin \phi_C \sin \phi_D \cos(60^\circ)$.
    *   $\phi_C$ is angle of $AC$ with $AB$.
    *   $\tan \phi_C = 2 / |z_C - z_A|$. Wait.
    *   Let $A$ be origin $(0,0,0)$.
    *   $C = (2\cos 60, 2\sin 60, z_c) = (1, \sqrt{3}, z_c)$.
    *   $\tan \phi_C = \frac{\sqrt{1^2+(\sqrt{3})^2}}{|z_c|} = \frac{2}{|z_c|}$.
    *   $\sin \phi_C = \frac{2}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}}$.
    *   $\cos \phi_C = \frac{|z_c|}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}}$.
    *   Similarly for $D$: $D = (1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   $\tan \phi_D = \frac{1}{|z_d|}$.
    *   $\sin \phi_D = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   $\cos \phi_D = \frac{|z_d|}{\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   We want to check $\cos \theta$.
    *   Note: The vectors $\vec{AC}$ and $\vec{AD}$ might point in "opposite" z-directions?
    *   If $z_c > 0$ and $z_d < 0$, then angle between vectors involves $\cos(\phi_C + \phi_D)$?
    *   Let's check the dot product formula directly.
    *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 1 + z_c z_d$.
    *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   If $z_c, z_d$ have opposite signs, $z_c z_d$ is negative.
    *   If $z_c z_d$ is very negative (large magnitude), $\cos \theta$ becomes negative $\implies \theta > 90^\circ$.
    *   So $\theta$ can be large.
    *   But option A says $\theta \ge 60^\circ$.
    *   We found a case where $\theta < 60^\circ$ (when $z_c, z_d$ same sign and large, or specific values).
    *   Example $z_c=1, z_d=1 \implies \cos \theta \approx 0.63 \implies \theta \approx 50.8^\circ < 60^\circ$.
    *   So A is definitely false.
    *   Wait, is "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$" signed?
    *   Usually dihedral angles are in $[0, 180]$. 60 is given.
    *   So relative position is fixed up to rotation/reflection.
    *   The calculation holds.

    *   **Double Check Option C logic.**
    *   Condition $AB \perp CD$.
    *   Vector $\vec{AB}$ is along $z$.
    *   $\vec{CD}$ must be perpendicular to $z$.
    *   So $z_c = z_d$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A, B, D$.
    *   $A, B$ on $z$-axis. $D$ at $(1, 0, z_d)$.

    *   Since it contains $z$-axis, equation is of form $ax + by = 0$.
    *   Passes through $D(1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   $a(1) + b(0) = 0 \implies a = 0$.
    *   So plane is $by = 0 \implies y=0$.
    *   So plane $ABD$ is the $xz$-plane.
    *   Normal vector is $(0, 1, 0)$.

    *   This vector is parallel to $(0, 1, 0)$.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is normal to plane $ABD$.
    *   So $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Correct.

    *   **Double Check Option D logic.**
    *   Condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.

    *   $\vec{AC} = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, 0)$.
    *   Dot product 1. Not 0.
    *   Wait, is it possible that $D$ is at angle $-60$ or something?
    *   Dihedral angle is 60.
    *   If $C$ is at 60, $D$ can be at 0 or 120?
    *   Angle between planes is 60.
    *   Planes are $P_C$ (containing $C$ and $z$) and $P_D$ (containing $D$ and $z$).
    *   Angle between them is 60.
    *   So angle between projection of $C$ and projection of $D$ on $xy$ plane is 60 or $120$?
    *   Dihedral angle is the angle between the planes.
    *   If we look at cross section perpendicular to $AB$, we see two rays. Angle is 60.
    *   So angle between vectors $\vec{OC}$ and $\vec{OD}$ (projections) is 60.
    *   Could it be 120?
    *   "Dihedral angle $C-AB-D$ is 60".
    *   Usually means the interior angle. If points are on opposite sides, maybe?
    *   But distance is always positive.
    *   Usually dihedral angle $\alpha \in [0, \pi]$.
    *   If it were 120, then angle between projections would be 120.
    *   If angle is 120:
    *   $C = (2\cos 60, 2\sin 60, 0) = (1, \sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $D = (1\cos 180, 1\sin 180, 0) = (-1, 0, 0)$? No, angle 120.
    *   $D = (-1/2, \sqrt{3}/2, 0)$?
    *   Dot product of projections: $2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 120 = -1$.
    *   Then $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = -1$ (if $z=0$).
    *   Then $\cos \theta = -1 / 2$. Angle 120.
    *   Still not 90.
    *   If angle between projections is 90? No, given 60.
    *   So D is robustly false.

    *   **Let's check Option B again.**
    *   $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   We found $CD^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
    *   Is it possible to have $CD < \sqrt{3}$?
    *   Only if $(z_c - z_d)^2$ is negative, impossible.
    *   Or if horizontal distance squared < 3.
    *   Horizontal distance squared = $|C_{xy} - D_{xy}|^2$.
    *   $C_{xy}$ length 2, $D_{xy}$ length 1.
    *   Angle $\gamma$ between them.
    *   Distance squared = $2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos \gamma = 5 - 4\cos \gamma$.
    *   Dihedral angle is 60.
    *   Does this imply $\gamma = 60$?
    *   Dihedral angle is angle between planes $ABC$ and $ABD$.
    *   Intersection is line $AB$.
    *   Take a point $P$ on $AB$. Draw lines in planes perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   In plane $ABC$, line perpendicular to $AB$ at $P$ is parallel to $C_{xy}$ vector?
    *   Yes, if $C_{xy}$ is projection.
    *   Actually, vector $\vec{PC}$ is not necessarily perpendicular to $AB$ unless $P$ is projection of $C$.
    *   But the direction of the plane is determined by the vector perpendicular to $AB$ in that plane.
    *   That vector is parallel to the projection of any vector in the plane (not parallel to $AB$) onto the perpendicular plane.
    *   So yes, the angle between the planes is the angle between the vectors $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ and $\vec{AD_{xy}}$?
    *   Wait, $\vec{AC}$ is in plane $ABC$. $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ is the component perpendicular to $AB$.
    *   So yes, the angle between the planes is the angle between $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ and $\vec{AD_{xy}}$.
    *   Is it possible that the angle is $360 - 60$? Or something?
    *   Dihedral angle is usually the smaller angle, so 60.
    *   So $\gamma = 60$ or $\gamma = -60$ (magnitude 60).
    *   $\cos 60 = 0.5$.
    *   So distance squared = $5 - 4(0.5) = 3$.
    *   So horizontal distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   So $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$.
    *   What if dihedral angle was defined differently? No, standard definition.
    *   What if $C$ and $D$ are on the same side of the plane passing through $AB$?
    *   No, that would be angle 0.
    *   What if they are on opposite sides of some reference?
    *   The angle between planes is 60.
    *   Could the angle between vectors be 120?
    *   If planes intersect at 60, the angle between normal vectors is 60 or 120.
    *   But angle between vectors lying in the planes and perpendicular to intersection line is 60.
    *   Are $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ and $\vec{AD_{xy}}$ perpendicular to $AB$?
    *   $AB$ is $z$-axis. $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ is in $xy$ plane. Yes, perpendicular.
    *   Wait, $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ is projection of $\vec{AC}$.
    *   Is $\vec{AC}$ in plane $ABC$? Yes.
    *   Is $\vec{AC_{xy}}$ in plane $ABC$?
    *   Plane $ABC$ contains $z$-axis and $C$.
    *   Any vector in plane $ABC$ has form $\lambda \vec{k} + \mu \vec{C}$.
    *   $\vec{C} = C_{xy} + z_c \vec{k}$.
    *   So $\vec{AC_{xy}} = C_{xy}$ is in the plane.
    *   Is it perpendicular to $AB$? Yes, $xy$ plane $\perp$ $z$ axis.
    *   So the angle between planes is exactly the angle between $C_{xy}$ and $D_{xy}$.
    *   So $\gamma = 60^\circ$ (or $300^\circ$).
    *   $\cos \gamma = 1/2$.
    *   So min distance is $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   So B is correct.

    *   **Is there any trick with "A, B fixed points"?**
    *   Maybe $A$ and $B$ are not on the line? No, "line AB".
    *   Maybe distance to line AB is not Euclidean? No.
    *   Maybe coordinates of A and B matter for $CD$?
    *   Distance $CD$ depends only on coordinates of $C$ and $D$.
    *   $C$ and $D$ constrained by distance to line and relative angle.
    *   Their $z$ coordinates are unconstrained (except relative to each other for min distance).
    *   So B holds.

    *   **Check Option C again.**
    *   "When $AB \perp CD$".
    *   We found this means $z_c = z_d$.
    *   "Then $CD \perp \text{plane } ABD$".
    *   We found this means $\vec{CD}$ is normal to plane.
    *   We verified $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AB} = 0$ (by condition) and $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0$.
    *   Wait, we checked $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0$?
    *   Let's re-verify that step carefully.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$ in our specific frame where $z_c=z_d=0$ (or any $z$).
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z_d)$.
    *   Dot product: $0(1) + (-\sqrt{3})(0) + 0(z_d) = 0$.
    *   Yes, it is 0.
    *   Wait, in the general frame?
    *   $C = (1, \sqrt{3}, z)$.
    *   $D = (1, 0, z)$. (Since $z_c=z_d=z$).
    *   $\vec{CD} = (0, -\sqrt{3}, 0)$.
    *   $A = (0,0,0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (1, 0, z)$.
    *   Dot product is 0.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$.
    *   Also $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AB}$ (since $\vec{AB}$ is along $z$, $\vec{CD}$ has 0 z-component).
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to two linearly independent vectors $\vec{AB}$ and $\vec{AD}$ in plane $ABD$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Yes, C is correct.
    *   Wait, did I assume $D$ is at angle 0?
    *   Let's check in general rotation.
    *   $D = (\cos \alpha, \sin \alpha, z)$.
    *   $C = (2\cos(\alpha+60), 2\sin(\alpha+60), z)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (\cos\alpha - 2\cos(\alpha+60), \sin\alpha - 2\sin(\alpha+60), 0)$.
    *   Using expansion:
    *   $\cos(\alpha+60) = \cos\alpha \cos60 - \sin\alpha \sin60 = \frac{1}{2}\cos\alpha - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\sin\alpha$.
    *   $\sin(\alpha+60) = \sin\alpha \cos60 + \cos\alpha \sin60 = \frac{1}{2}\sin\alpha + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\cos\alpha$.
    *   $x$-comp: $\cos\alpha - 2(\frac{1}{2}\cos\alpha - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\sin\alpha) = \cos\alpha - \cos\alpha + \sqrt{3}\sin\alpha = \sqrt{3}\sin\alpha$.
    *   $y$-comp: $\sin\alpha - 2(\frac{1}{2}\sin\alpha + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\cos\alpha) = \sin\alpha - \sin\alpha - \sqrt{3}\cos\alpha = -\sqrt{3}\cos\alpha$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} = (\sqrt{3}\sin\alpha, -\sqrt{3}\cos\alpha, 0)$.
    *   Now check dot product with $\vec{AD} = (\cos\alpha, \sin\alpha, z)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = \sqrt{3}\sin\alpha \cos\alpha - \sqrt{3}\cos\alpha \sin\alpha + 0 = 0$.
    *   Yes!
    *   Check dot product with $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$.
    *   $0$.
    *   So $\vec{CD}$ is perpendicular to both.
    *   So C is robustly correct.

    *   Wait, is it a multiple choice question with single or multiple answers?
    *   "多选题" means Multiple Choice Question (Select all that apply).

    *   Let's double check if there's any edge case or misinterpretation.
    *   Maybe "Distance to line AB" means distance from C to the segment AB?
    *   If distance to segment, and projection falls outside, distance is to endpoint.
    *   But "直线AB" is explicitly line.
    *   Maybe "Dihedral angle" implies specific orientation?
    *   Even so, B and C held regardless of rotation $\alpha$.
    *   Maybe A is true?
    *   Let's check if I missed a constraint.
    *   Maybe $A$ and $B$ order matters? No.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ must be on same side of plane? No.
    *   Maybe $C, D$ distance to $AB$ is minimal distance? Yes.
    *   Is it possible that $\angle CAD$ is always $\ge 60$?
    *   Let's check the dot product again.
    *   $\cos \theta = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   We found case $z_c=1, z_d=1$ gives $\cos \theta \approx 0.63$.
    *   Is it possible that $C$ and $D$ cannot be at $z=1$?
    *   $C$ is distance 2 from line. $C=(1, \sqrt{3}, 1)$. Distance $\sqrt{1+3}=2$. OK.
    *   $D$ is distance 1 from line. $D=(1, 0, 1)$. Distance $\sqrt{1}=1$. OK.
    *   Angle between planes: Normal to $ABC$ is $\vec{n}_C = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AC} = (0,0,1) \times (1, \sqrt{3}, 1) = (-\sqrt{3}, 1, 0)$.
    *   Normal to $ABD$ is $\vec{n}_D = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (0,0,1) \times (1, 0, 1) = (0, 1, 0)$.
    *   Angle between normals: $\cos \phi = \frac{\vec{n}_C \cdot \vec{n}_D}{|\vec{n}_C||\vec{n}_D|} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3+1} \cdot 1} = \frac{1}{2}$.
    *   So angle is 60.
    *   So configuration is valid.
    *   $\angle CAD$ was $\approx 50.8^\circ$.
    *   So A is definitely false.

    *   Maybe D is true under some interpretation?
    *   "When $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$".
    *   This forces $AC \perp AB$ and $AD \perp AB$.
    *   So $AC$ and $AD$ are in the plane perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   In this plane, $C$ is at distance 2 from $A$, $D$ is at distance 1 from $A$.
    *   Angle between $AC$ and $AD$ is determined by dihedral angle.
    *   Is the angle between lines $AC$ and $AD$ in 3D equal to the dihedral angle?
    *   If both lines are perpendicular to the intersection line $AB$ at the same point $A$, then yes, the angle between the lines is the dihedral angle.
    *   Here $AC \perp AB$ (since $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$).
    *   And $AD \perp AB$ (since $AB \perp$ plane $ACD$).
    *   So $AC$ and $AD$ are both perpendicular to $AB$ at $A$.
    *   Therefore, the angle $\angle CAD$ is exactly the dihedral angle between plane $ABC$ (containing $AC$) and plane $ABD$ (containing $AD$).
    *   Wait, plane $ABC$ is plane containing $A, B, C$. Since $AC \perp AB$, this plane is well defined and contains the line $AC$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A, B, D$. Since $AD \perp AB$, this plane is well defined and contains line $AD$.
    *   The dihedral angle is the angle between these planes.
    *   Since $AC$ and $AD$ are lines in these planes perpendicular to the intersection line $AB$ at the same point $A$, the angle between $AC$ and $AD$ *is* the dihedral angle.
    *   The problem states dihedral angle is $60^\circ$.
    *   Therefore, $\angle CAD = 60^\circ$.
    *   The option D claims $AC \perp AD$, which implies $\angle CAD = 90^\circ$.
    *   $60^\circ \ne 90^\circ$.
    *   So D is false.

    *   Wait, is it possible for the dihedral angle to be defined differently?
    *   Dihedral angle between two half-planes.
    *   Maybe $C$ and $D$ are on opposite sides?
    *   If they are on opposite sides, angle could be 120?
    *   But usually "dihedral angle is 60" implies the measure is 60.
    *   Even if it was 120, $120 \ne 90$.
    *   Is it possible the distance 2 and 1 allows for a specific geometry where angle is 90?
    *   No, angle is fixed by the condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$.
    *   This condition rigidly fixes the geometry of the angle at $A$.
    *   So D is definitely false.

    *   **Is B definitely true?**
    *   $CD^2 = 3 + (z_c - z_d)^2$.
    *   Is it possible $z_c \ne z_d$ is impossible?
    *   No constraints link $z_c$ and $z_d$.
    *   Wait, if $A, B$ are fixed points.
    *   Maybe $C, D$ are restricted to be "between" A and B?
    *   No, "moving point" usually implies anywhere in space satisfying condition.
    *   Maybe distance to *segment* AB?
    *   If distance to segment, and $z$ is outside range $[z_A, z_B]$, distance is to endpoint.
    *   But text says "直线AB" (Line AB).
    *   So B is correct.

    *   **Is C definitely true?**
    *   We proved it analytically.
    *   Let's do a geometric intuition check.
    *   $AB \perp CD$ means $CD$ is perpendicular to the axis.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ are at same "height" (same projection on line).
    *   Let's unfold the planes?
    *   Or look from top.
    *   $C$ and $D$ are in the same horizontal plane.
    *   $C$ is at distance 2, $D$ at distance 1.
    *   Angle between their radial vectors is 60.
    *   Triangle $OCD$ (O is origin on axis). Sides $OC=2, OD=1$, angle 60.
    *   By cosine rule, $CD^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 - 2(2)(1)\cos 60 = 3$.
    *   So $CD = \sqrt{3}$.
    *   Also $CD$ is horizontal.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains vertical axis and point $D$.
    *   Is $CD$ perpendicular to this plane?
    *   In the horizontal plane (top view), plane $ABD$ projects to the line $OD$.
    *   Vector $CD$ connects $C$ to $D$.
    *   We need $CD \perp OD$?
    *   In $\triangle OCD$, sides 2, 1, $\sqrt{3}$.
    *   Check if right triangle.
    *   $1^2 + (\sqrt{3})^2 = 1 + 3 = 4 = 2^2$.
    *   Yes, $OD^2 + CD^2 = OC^2$.
    *   So angle at $D$ is 90 degrees.
    *   So $CD \perp OD$.
    *   Since $CD$ is horizontal and $OD$ is the trace of plane $ABD$ on horizontal plane, and $CD \perp OD$, and $CD \perp$ vertical axis (since horizontal), $CD$ is perpendicular to plane $ABD$.
    *   Geometric intuition confirms C.

    *   **Wait, check the order of vertices in D.**
    *   "AB \perp plane ACD".
    *   This means line AB is normal to plane ACD.
    *   This implies AB is perpendicular to every line in ACD passing through intersection.
    *   Intersection is A.
    *   So AB $\perp$ AC and AB $\perp$ AD.
    *   So angle between AC and AD is the dihedral angle?
    *   Plane ABC contains AB and AC. Since AC $\perp$ AB, this plane is perpendicular to AB? No.
    *   Plane ABC contains a line (AC) perpendicular to AB.
    *   Plane ABD contains a line (AD) perpendicular to AB.
    *   The angle between the planes is the angle between these perpendiculars (AC and AD).

    *   No, that's the definition.

    *   Vertices $O, C, D$.

    *   **Is it possible for Option A to be true?**
    *   We found counterexample.

    *   Actually $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,10)$ say.

    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and $D(1, 0, 1)$.

    *   $\angle CAD$:
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$.

    *   Is it possible that the dihedral angle constraint restricts $z$?

    *   As long as $C$ and $D$ are not on $AB$, planes are defined.

    *   **Let's write down the solution steps clearly.**
    *   1. Define coordinate system. $A(0,0,0)$, Line $AB$ is $z$-axis.
    *   2. Express coordinates of $C$ and $D$ based on distance constraints and dihedral angle.
        *   $C(2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, z_c)$.
        *   $D(\cos(\theta+60^\circ), \sin(\theta+60^\circ), z_d)$.
        *   (Assuming angle difference is 60).
    *   3. Analyze Option A. Calculate $\cos \angle CAD$. Find counterexample.
    *   4. Analyze Option B. Calculate distance $CD$. Find minimum.
    *   5. Analyze Option C. Apply condition $AB \perp CD$ ($z_c=z_d$). Check perpendicularity to plane $ABD$.
    *   6. Analyze Option D. Apply condition $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD$ ($z_c=z_d=0$). Check angle $\angle CAD$.

    *   **Refining Step 3 (Option A):**
    *   $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{2\cos\theta \cos(\theta+60) + 2\sin\theta \sin(\theta+60) + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   Numerator term $x_c x_d + y_c y_d = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos(60) = 1$.
    *   So $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}}$.
    *   We need to check if this is $\le 1/2$ (for angle $\ge 60$).
    *   Check if $\frac{1 + z_c z_d}{\sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}} \le \frac{1}{2}$.
    *   $2(1 + z_c z_d) \le \sqrt{4+z_c^2}\sqrt{1+z_d^2}$.
    *   Square both sides (assuming LHS positive, if negative inequality holds).
    *   $4(1 + 2z_c z_d + z_c^2 z_d^2) \le (4+z_c^2)(1+z_d^2) = 4 + 4z_d^2 + z_c^2 + z_c^2 z_d^2$.
    *   $4 + 8z_c z_d + 4z_c^2 z_d^2 \le 4 + 4z_d^2 + z_c^2 + z_c^2 z_d^2$.
    *   $3z_c^2 z_d^2 + 8z_c z_d - 4z_d^2 - z_c^2 \le 0$.
    *   Let $u = z_c, v = z_d$.
    *   $3u^2 v^2 + 8uv - 4v^2 - u^2 \le 0$.
    *   Treat as quadratic in $v$? Or just plug in numbers.
    *   We found $u=1, v=1$.
    *   $3(1)(1) + 8(1) - 4(1) - 1 = 3 + 8 - 4 - 1 = 6 > 0$.
    *   Inequality fails.
    *   So $\cos > 1/2$ is possible.
    *   So Angle $< 60$ is possible.
    *   Option A False.

    *   **Refining Step 4 (Option B):**
    *   $CD^2 = (x_c-x_d)^2 + (y_c-y_d)^2 + (z_c-z_d)^2$.
    *   $(x_c-x_d)^2 + (y_c-y_d)^2 = x_c^2+y_c^2 + x_d^2+y_d^2 - 2(x_c x_d + y_c y_d)$.
    *   $= 4 + 1 - 2(1) = 3$.
    *   $CD^2 = 3 + (z_c-z_d)^2 \ge 3$.
    *   Option B True.

    *   **Refining Step 5 (Option C):**
    *   $AB \perp CD \implies \vec{AB} \cdot \vec{CD} = 0$.
    *   $\vec{AB} = (0,0,1)$. $\vec{CD} = (x_d-x_c, y_d-y_c, z_d-z_c)$.
    *   $z_d - z_c = 0 \implies z_c = z_d$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains vectors $\vec{AB}=(0,0,1)$ and $\vec{AD}=(x_d, y_d, z_d)$.
    *   Normal $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (-y_d, x_d, 0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (x_d-x_c, y_d-y_c, 0)$.
    *   We need $\vec{CD} \parallel \vec{n}$.
    *   Check cross product $\vec{CD} \times \vec{n} = 0$? Or dot products.
    *   We need $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AB} = 0$ (True, $z$-diff 0) and $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0$.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = x_d(x_d-x_c) + y_d(y_d-y_c) + z_d(0)$.
    *   $= x_d^2 + y_d^2 - (x_d x_c + y_d y_c)$.
    *   $x_d^2 + y_d^2 = 1$ (distance of D is 1).
    *   $x_d x_c + y_d y_c = 1$ (dot product of projections, $2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos 60$).
    *   So $1 - 1 = 0$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$.
    *   Since $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AB}$ and $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$, and $AB, AD$ span plane $ABD$, $\vec{CD} \perp \text{plane } ABD$.
    *   Option C True.

    *   **Refining Step 6 (Option D):**
    *   $AB \perp \text{plane } ACD \implies AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   $AB$ is $z$-axis.
    *   So $C$ and $D$ must have $z$-coordinate 0 (since $A$ is origin).
    *   $C = (2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, 0)$.
    *   $D = (1\cos(\theta+60), 1\sin(\theta+60), 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} = (2\cos\theta, 2\sin\theta, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (\cos(\theta+60), \sin(\theta+60), 0)$.
    *   Dot product $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 2 \cdot 1 \cdot \cos(60) = 1$.
    *   Lengths 2 and 1.
    *   $\cos \angle CAD = 1/2$.
    *   Angle is 60.
    *   $AC \not\perp AD$.
    *   Option D False.

    *   **Conclusion:** B and C are correct.

    *   **Wait, is it possible that "A, B fixed points" implies a specific distance AB?**
    *   No, "fixed points" just means they don't move. Their distance is constant but arbitrary.

    *   Wait, let me double check the calculation for Option C again.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = 0$.
    *   $D = (x_d, y_d, z)$. $C = (x_c, y_c, z)$.
    *   $\vec{AD} = (x_d, y_d, z)$.
    *   $\vec{CD} = (x_d-x_c, y_d-y_c, 0)$.
    *   Dot product: $x_d(x_d-x_c) + y_d(y_d-y_c) = x_d^2 + y_d^2 - (x_d x_c + y_d y_c)$.
    *   $x_d^2 + y_d^2 = 1^2 = 1$.
    *   $x_d x_c + y_d y_c = \vec{D_{xy}} \cdot \vec{C_{xy}} = 1 \cdot 2 \cdot \cos 60^\circ = 1$.
    *   Result $1 - 1 = 0$.
    *   Calculation is robust.
    *   Wait, is $\vec{D_{xy}} \cdot \vec{C_{xy}}$ definitely 1?
    *   $|\vec{D_{xy}}| = 1$. $|\vec{C_{xy}}| = 2$.
    *   Angle is 60.
    *   Dot product $1 \cdot 2 \cdot \cos 60 = 1$.
    *   Yes.
    *   Is it possible angle is -60?
    *   $\cos(-60) = 1/2$. Dot product still 1.
    *   So yes.

    *   Dihedral angle is 60.
    *   Dihedral angle is the angle between the planes.
    *   The planes are vertical planes passing through the origin (if A is origin).
    *   The angle between vertical planes is the angle between their traces on the horizontal plane.
    *   The traces are the lines containing $C_{xy}$ and $D_{xy}$?
    *   No.
    *   Plane $ABC$ contains $z$-axis and $C$.
    *   Intersection with $xy$-plane is the line passing through origin and $C_{xy}$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $z$-axis and $D$.
    *   Intersection with $xy$-plane is the line passing through origin and $D_{xy}$.
    *   So the angle between planes is exactly the angle between vectors $\vec{OC_{xy}}$ and $\vec{OD_{xy}}$.
    *   Wait, $\vec{OC_{xy}}$ is vector from origin to projection of C.
    *   $C = (x_c, y_c, z_c)$. Projection is $(x_c, y_c, 0)$.
    *   Vector is $(x_c, y_c, 0)$.
    *   Length is distance to z-axis, which is 2.
    *   Same for D.
    *   So yes, angle between vectors $(x_c, y_c)$ and $(x_d, y_d)$ is 60.
    *   So dot product is 1.
    *   Everything holds.

    *   Let's check if Option D could be interpreted differently.
    *   Maybe $AC \perp AD$ is not about angle at A?
    *   "$\perp$" for lines means angle 90.
    *   Yes.

    *   So, B and C.

    *   Wait, I should check if A, B, C, D options are mutually exclusive or if it's a "Select all correct" type.
    *   "多选题" usually means select all correct options.
    *   So listing B, C is appropriate.

    *   Let's write down the final argument.
    *   Establish coordinates.
    *   $A=(0,0,0)$, $B=(0,0,1)$ (direction). Line is z-axis.
    *   $C=(2\cos\alpha, 2\sin\alpha, h_1)$.
    *   $D=(\cos(\alpha+60^\circ), \sin(\alpha+60^\circ), h_2)$. (Rotated frame).
    *   Or simply $D=(\cos\beta, \sin\beta, h_2)$ with $|\alpha-\beta|=60$.
    *   Check A: $\vec{AC}\cdot\vec{AD} = 2\cos\alpha\cos\beta + 2\sin\alpha\sin\beta + h_1 h_2 = 2\cos(\alpha-\beta) + h_1 h_2 = 1 + h_1 h_2$.
    *   $|\vec{AC}| = \sqrt{4+h_1^2}$, $|\vec{AD}| = \sqrt{1+h_2^2}$.
    *   $\cos \angle CAD = \frac{1+h_1 h_2}{\sqrt{4+h_1^2}\sqrt{1+h_2^2}}$.
    *   If $h_1=h_2=1$, $\cos = 2/\sqrt{10} > 1/2$, so angle $< 60$. A is False.
    *   Check B: $CD^2 = (2\cos\alpha-\cos\beta)^2 + (2\sin\alpha-\sin\beta)^2 + (h_1-h_2)^2$.
    *   Horizontal part: $4+1 - 4\cos(\alpha-\beta) = 5 - 4(1/2) = 3$.
    *   $CD^2 = 3 + (h_1-h_2)^2 \ge 3$. $CD \ge \sqrt{3}$. B is True.
    *   Check C: $AB \perp CD \implies (0,0,1) \cdot (D-C) = 0 \implies h_1=h_2$.
    *   Plane $ABD$ contains $A(0,0,0)$, $B(0,0,1)$, $D(\cos\beta, \sin\beta, h_1)$.
    *   Normal $\vec{n} = \vec{AB} \times \vec{AD} = (0,0,1) \times (\cos\beta, \sin\beta, h_1) = (-\sin\beta, \cos\beta, 0)$.
    *   Vector $\vec{CD} = (\cos\beta-2\cos\alpha, \sin\beta-2\sin\alpha, 0)$.
    *   We need $\vec{CD} \parallel \vec{n}$.
    *   Check cross product or proportionality.
    *   Actually check dot product with basis vectors of plane $ABD$.
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AB} = 0$ (since z-diff 0).
    *   $\vec{CD} \cdot \vec{AD} = (\cos\beta-2\cos\alpha)\cos\beta + (\sin\beta-2\sin\alpha)\sin\beta + 0$.
    *   $= \cos^2\beta + \sin^2\beta - 2(\cos\alpha\cos\beta + \sin\alpha\sin\beta)$.
    *   $= 1 - 2\cos(\alpha-\beta) = 1 - 2(1/2) = 0$.
    *   So $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AD}$ and $\vec{CD} \perp \vec{AB}$.
    *   Thus $\vec{CD} \perp$ Plane $ABD$. C is True.
    *   Check D: $AB \perp$ Plane $ACD \implies AB \perp AC$ and $AB \perp AD$.
    *   $\implies h_1=0, h_2=0$ (since A is origin).
    *   Then $\vec{AC} = (2\cos\alpha, 2\sin\alpha, 0)$, $\vec{AD} = (\cos\beta, \sin\beta, 0)$.
    *   $\vec{AC} \cdot \vec{AD} = 2\cos(\alpha-\beta) = 1$.
    *   $|\vec{AC}||\vec{AD}| = 2 \cdot 1 = 2$.
    *   $\cos \angle CAD = 1/2 \implies 60^\circ$.
    *   Not 90. D is False.

    *   The logic is flawless.
